=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given three integers m, n, and k.
There is a rectangular grid of size m × n containing k identical pieces. Return the sum of Manhattan distances between every pair of pieces over all valid arrangements of pieces.
A valid arrangement is a placement of all k pieces on the grid with at most one piece per cell.
Since the answer may be very large, return it modulo 10^9 + 7.
The Manhattan Distance between two cells (x_i, y_i) and (x_j, y_j) is |x_i - x_j| + |y_i - y_j|.
 
Example 1:

Input: m = 2, n = 2, k = 2
Output: 8
Explanation:
The valid arrangements of pieces on the board are:


In the first 4 arrangements, the Manhattan distance between the two pieces is 1.
In the last 2 arrangements, the Manhattan distance between the two pieces is 2.

Thus, the total Manhattan distance across all valid arrangements is 1 + 1 + 1 + 1 + 2 + 2 = 8.

Example 2:

Input: m = 1, n = 4, k = 3
Output: 20
Explanation:
The valid arrangements of pieces on the board are:


The first and last arrangements have a total Manhattan distance of 1 + 1 + 2 = 4.
The middle two arrangements have a total Manhattan distance of 1 + 2 + 3 = 6.

The total Manhattan distance between all pairs of pieces across all arrangements is 4 + 6 + 6 + 4 = 20.

 
Constraints:

1 <= m, n <= 10^5
2 <= m * n <= 10^5
2 <= k <= m * n

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def distanceSum(self, m: int, n: int, k: int) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Pseudo:

MOD = 10**9+7
class Solution:
    def distanceSum(self, m:int,n:int,k:int)->int:
        N=m*n
        # precompute fact/invfact up to N
        fact=[1]*(N+1)
        for i in range(1,N+1):
            fact[i]=fact[i-1]*i%MOD
        invfact=[1]*(N+1)
        invfact[N]=pow(fact[N], MOD-2, MOD)
        for i in range(N,0,-1):
            invfact[i-1]=invfact[i]*i%MOD
        def C(a,b):
            if b<0 or b>a: return 0
            return fact[a]*invfact[b]%MOD*invfact[a-b]%MOD
        comb=C(N-2,k-2)
        inv6=pow(6, MOD-2, MOD)
        term1 = (n*n % MOD) * m % MOD * ((m*m -1) % MOD) % MOD
        term2 = (m*m % MOD) * n % MOD * ((n*n -1) % MOD) % MOD
        S = (term1 + term2) % MOD * inv6 % MOD
        return comb * S % MOD

Let's ensure code style:

class Solution:
    def distanceSum(self, m: int, n: int, k: int) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        N = m * n

        fact = [1] * (N + 1)
        for i in range(1, N + 1):
            fact[i] = fact[i-1] * i % MOD

        inv_fact = [1] * (N + 1)
        inv_fact[N] = pow(fact[N], MOD - 2, MOD)
        for i in range(N, 0, -1):
            inv_fact[i-1] = inv_fact[i] * i % MOD

        def comb(a, b):
            if b < 0 or b > a:
                return 0
            return fact[a] * inv_fact[b] % MOD * inv_fact[a-b] % MOD

        ways = comb(N - 2, k - 2)

        inv6 = pow(6, MOD - 2, MOD)
        term_x = (n * n % MOD) * m % MOD * ((m * m - 1) % MOD) % MOD
        term_y = (m * m % MOD) * n % MOD * ((n * n - 1) % MOD) % MOD
        pair_sum = (term_x + term_y) % MOD * inv6 % MOD

        return ways * pair_sum % MOD

            if b < 0 or b > a: return 0

        pair_sum = (sx + sy) % MOD * inv6 % MOD
        return ways * pair_sum % MOD

Let's write final code:

```
MOD = 10**9 + 7
MAX_CELLS = 100000

fact = [1] * (MAX_CELLS + 1)
for i in range(1, MAX_CELLS + 1):
    fact[i] = fact[i - 1] * i % MOD

inv_fact = [1] * (MAX_CELLS + 1)
inv_fact[MAX_CELLS] = pow(fact[MAX_CELLS], MOD - 2, MOD)
for i in range(MAX_CELLS, 0, -1):
    inv_fact[i - 1] = inv_fact[i] * i % MOD

INV6 = pow(6, MOD - 2, MOD)

class Solution:
    def distanceSum(self, m: int, n: int, k: int) -> int:
        N = m * n

        def comb(a: int, b: int) -> int:
            if b < 0 or b > a:
                return 0
            return fact[a] * inv_fact[b] % MOD * inv_fact[a - b] % MOD

        ways = comb(N - 2, k - 2)

        row_dist = (m - 1) * m % MOD * (m + 1) % MOD * INV6 % MOD
        col_dist = (n - 1) * n % MOD * (n + 1) % MOD * INV6 % MOD

        pair_sum = (n * n % MOD * row_dist + m * m % MOD * col_dist) % MOD

        return ways * pair_sum % MOD
```

```
class Solution:
    _fact = [1]
    _inv_fact = [1]

    @classmethod
    def _build(cls, N):
        if N < len(cls._fact):
            return
        old = len(cls._fact)
        fact = cls._fact + [0]*(N+1-old)
        for i in range(old, N+1):
            fact[i] = fact[i-1]*i%MOD
        cls._fact = fact
        inv_fact = [1]*(N+1)
        inv_fact[N] = pow(fact[N], MOD-2, MOD)
        for i in range(N,0,-1):
            inv_fact[i-1] = inv_fact[i]*i%MOD
        cls._inv_fact = inv_fact
```
But this recomputes inv_fact from scratch each time N grows, okay. But if first call N=100000, fine. If second call N=50000, uses existing. This is robust and no global precompute. But more complex. Not necessary.

The problem constraints are small enough. Let's choose inside method to keep code exactly class. The final answer code block can be:

```
class Solution:
    def distanceSum(self, m: int, n: int, k: int) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        N = m * n

        fact = [1] * (N + 1)
        for i in range(1, N + 1):
            fact[i] = fact[i - 1] * i % MOD

        inv_fact = [1] * (N + 1)
        inv_fact[N] = pow(fact[N], MOD - 2, MOD)
        for i in range(N, 0, -1):
            inv_fact[i - 1] = inv_fact[i] * i % MOD

        def comb(a, b):
            if b < 0 or b > a:
                return 0
            return fact[a] * inv_fact[b] % MOD * inv_fact[a - b] % MOD

        ways = comb(N - 2, k - 2)

        inv6 = pow(6, MOD - 2, MOD)
        row_dist = (m - 1) * m % MOD * (m + 1) % MOD * inv6 % MOD
        col_dist = (n - 1) * n % MOD * (n + 1) % MOD * inv6 % MOD

        pair_sum = (n * n % MOD * row_dist + m * m % MOD * col_dist) % MOD

        return ways * pair_sum % MOD
```

This is self-contained and uses N. If multiple calls, recomputes. But okay. Let's maybe optimize by using `math.comb`? No.

MOD = 10**9 + 7
MAX_CELLS = 100000

fact = [1] * (MAX_CELLS + 1)
for i in range(1, MAX_CELLS + 1):
    fact[i] = fact[i - 1] * i % MOD

inv_fact = [1] * (MAX_CELLS + 1)
inv_fact[MAX_CELLS] = pow(fact[MAX_CELLS], MOD - 2, MOD)
for i in range(MAX_CELLS, 0, -1):
    inv_fact[i - 1] = inv_fact[i] * i % MOD

INV6 = pow(6, MOD - 2, MOD)

class Solution:
    def distanceSum(self, m: int, n: int, k: int) -> int:
        N = m * n

        def comb(a: int, b: int) -> int:
            if b < 0 or b > a:
                return 0
            return fact[a] * inv_fact[b] % MOD * inv_fact[a - b] % MOD

        ways = comb(N - 2, k - 2)

        row_dist = (m - 1) * m % MOD * (m + 1) % MOD * INV6 % MOD
        col_dist = (n - 1) * n % MOD * (n + 1) % MOD * INV6 % MOD

        pair_sum = (n * n % MOD * row_dist + m * m % MOD * col_dist) % MOD

        return ways * pair_sum % MOD
```

Let's derive: For each arrangement missing one cell, sum distances among remaining = S_total - sum distances from missing cell to all others. Sum over missing cell = N*S_total - 2*S_total? Because each unordered pair distance is subtracted when missing cell is one of the two; for each pair, there are 2 missing choices out of N. So total = N*S_total - 2*S_total = (N-2)*S_total. Our formula C(N-2,k-2)=C(N-2,N-3)=N-2. Yes. Good.

Let's maybe produce final code now. But before final, let's re-evaluate the mathematical derivation one more time to ensure no missing factor.

Total sum over all valid arrangements of pairwise distances:
Let A be set of all k-subsets of cells. For each arrangement A, D(A)=Σ_{unordered pairs {u,v}⊆A} dist(u,v). Total T=Σ_A D(A)=Σ_{unordered cell pairs {u,v}} dist(u,v) * (# arrangements containing both u,v). # arrangements containing both = C(N-2,k-2). Thus T = C(N-2,k-2) * Σ_{all unordered cell pairs} dist(u,v). Yes.

Now S = Σ_{all unordered cell pairs} (|Δx|+|Δy|) = Σ |Δx| + Σ |Δy|.
For x coordinate with m rows:
Σ over all unordered cell pairs |Δx| = Σ_{r1<r2} (r2-r1) * n * n. Because for each row pair, choose any y in row r1 (n choices) and any y in row r2 (n choices). This counts unordered cell pairs with different rows. If same row, |Δx|=0. So n^2 * Σ_{d=1}^{m-1} d*(m-d). Σ d(m-d) = mΣd - Σd^2 = m*m(m-1)/2 - m(m-1)(2m-1)/6 = m(m-1)(m+1)/6. Yes. So S_x = n^2 * m(m-1)(m+1)/6. Similarly S_y = m^2 * n(n-1)(n+1)/6. Good.

Let's test with m=1,n=4: S_x=16*0=0, S_y=1*4*3*5/6=10. Good.
m=2,n=2: S_x=4*2*1*3/6=4, S_y=4. Good.
m=3,n=3: S_x=9*3*2*4/6=36, S_y=36, S=72. Let's brute maybe 3x3 all unordered pairs distances: For each row pair diff1: 2 row pairs *9=18, diff2:1*9=9 => S_x=27? Wait formula m=3: m(m-1)(m+1)/6=3*2*4/6=4, n^2=9 =>36. But let's compute row pairs: rows 0,1 diff1: n^2=9; rows1,2 diff1:9; rows0,2 diff2:18; total36. Yes. S_y=36. Total72. Good.

Now, combination C(N-2,k-2) with N=9,k=2 => C7,0=1, answer72. All unordered pairs in 3x3 sum distances? Let's quick compute horizontal/vertical/diagonal maybe 72 plausible. Good.

Let's decide final code. I will include global precompute. But maybe to be safe with "starter code", I can put precomputation inside method but use a static cache? Let's consider implementing a class-level cache without global top-level loops, to be both self-contained and efficient. For example:

```
class Solution:
    _fact = [1]
    _inv_fact = [1]

    @classmethod
    def _prepare(cls, n):
        if n < len(cls._fact):
            return
        fact = cls._fact + [0] * (n + 1 - len(cls._fact))
        for i in range(len(cls._fact), n + 1):
            fact[i] = fact[i-1] * i % MOD
        cls._fact = fact
        inv_fact = [1] * (n + 1)
        inv_fact[n] = pow(fact[n], MOD - 2, MOD)
        for i in range(n, 0, -1):
            inv_fact[i-1] = inv_fact[i] * i % MOD
        cls._inv_fact = inv_fact
```
But this recomputes inv_fact from scratch whenever n grows. If first call n=100000, fine. If first call n=50000, then second call n=100000, inv_fact recomputed from 100000 down to 0, okay. But if first call n=100000, second call n=50000, uses existing. This is robust and no top-level loops. But more code and potential bugs. Global precompute simpler.

```

Let's ensure code indentation and no syntax errors:
```
MOD = 10**9 + 7
MAX_CELLS = 100000

fact = [1] * (MAX_CELLS + 1)
for i in range(1, MAX_CELLS + 1):
    fact[i] = fact[i - 1] * i % MOD

inv_fact = [1] * (MAX_CELLS + 1)
inv_fact[MAX_CELLS] = pow(fact[MAX_CELLS], MOD - 2, MOD)
for i in range(MAX_CELLS, 0, -1):
    inv_fact[i - 1] = inv_fact[i] * i % MOD

INV6 = pow(6, MOD - 2, MOD)

class Solution:
    def distanceSum(self, m: int, n: int, k: int) -> int:
        N = m * n

        def comb(a: int, b: int) -> int:
            if b < 0 or b > a:
                return 0
            return fact[a] * inv_fact[b] % MOD * inv_fact[a - b] % MOD

        ways = comb(N - 2, k - 2)

        row_dist = (m - 1) * m % MOD * (m + 1) % MOD * INV6 % MOD
        col_dist = (n - 1) * n % MOD * (n + 1) % MOD * INV6 % MOD

        pair_sum = (n * n % MOD * row_dist + m * m % MOD * col_dist) % MOD

        return ways * pair_sum % MOD
```

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "fact" and "inv_fact" if `MAX_CELLS` is 100000 but if m*n = 100000 and N-2 = 99998, fact[99998] computed. Good.

Let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "answer" if m=100000,n=1,k=2, row_dist mod. Good.

Let's consider if there is any issue with "answer" if m=100000,n=1,k=100000, ways=1. Good.

Let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "answer" if m=100000,n=1,k=99999, ways=99998. Good.

</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
MOD = 10**9 + 7
MAX_CELLS = 100000

fact = [1] * (MAX_CELLS + 1)
for i in range(1, MAX_CELLS + 1):
    fact[i] = fact[i - 1] * i % MOD

inv_fact = [1] * (MAX_CELLS + 1)
inv_fact[MAX_CELLS] = pow(fact[MAX_CELLS], MOD - 2, MOD)
for i in range(MAX_CELLS, 0, -1):
    inv_fact[i - 1] = inv_fact[i] * i % MOD

INV6 = pow(6, MOD - 2, MOD)


class Solution:
    def distanceSum(self, m: int, n: int, k: int) -> int:
        N = m * n

        def comb(a: int, b: int) -> int:
            if b < 0 or b > a:
                return 0
            return fact[a] * inv_fact[b] % MOD * inv_fact[a - b] % MOD

        ways = comb(N - 2, k - 2)

        row_dist = (m - 1) * m % MOD * (m + 1) % MOD * INV6 % MOD
        col_dist = (n - 1) * n % MOD * (n + 1) % MOD * INV6 % MOD

        pair_sum = (n * n % MOD * row_dist + m * m % MOD * col_dist) % MOD

        return ways * pair_sum % MOD
```
